2005

The door opens, and Agatha’s head snaps toward the sound just in time to see Rio slipping inside the dorm, nudging the door shut behind her with her foot. The room smells faintly like laundry detergent and old books, the hum of the hallway disappearing as the latch clicks closed.

“Hey, my love,” Rio says, dropping her bag by the door before crossing the room. She leans down and presses a soft kiss to Agatha’s cheek, lingering just long enough to make Agatha smile.

Agatha pushes herself upright on the bed, one hand instinctively resting on her stomach. “Look at the new shirt I got,” she says, pride bright in her voice.

Rio’s gaze drops to the oversized t-shirt draped over Agatha’s pregnant body- ABBA printed boldly across the front, slightly wrinkled from being worn straight out of the bag. She lets out a quiet laugh, shaking her head.

“Your obsession is gonna put us in debt.”

“It was, like, ten dollars,” Agatha groans, rolling her eyes dramatically.

“Ten dollars that could’ve gone toward food, or baby clothes, or-”

“Okay, okay, I get it.” Agatha cuts her off with a wave of her hand, but then her expression shifts, uncertainty flickering through the smile. “You don’t like it?”

Rio exhales softly, disbelief and affection tangling together. “Of course I like it,” she says, voice lowering. “Mi niña linda.”

My pretty girl.

She climbs onto the loft bed, the frame creaking faintly beneath her weight, and scoots in beside Agatha, curling instinctively into her space like it’s muscle memory. Agatha leans into her immediately, resting her head against Rio’s shoulder, warm and solid and safe.

“Play our song?” she whispers.

Rio smiles, reaching for her iPod, finding the song, and pressing play. Andante, Andante crackles to life, the sound thin but familiar, filling the small room anyway. She places it gently on her stomach, right beside Agatha’s belly.

“So Nicky can hear it too,” she murmurs.

The lyrics spill out softly.

“Take it easy with me, please

Touch me gently like a summer evening breeze

Take your time, make it slow”

Rio’s lips curl into a small smile as she joins in, singing the words under her breath, just for Agatha.

“Andante, Andante

Just let the feeling grow”

Rio runs her fingers through Agatha’s hair, slow and patient, untangling the day from her. Agatha’s breathing evens out against her chest, growing heavier with each pass. Rio tilts her head, watching her eyelashes flutter.

“Are you falling asleep?” she whispers into her hair.

Agatha hums in response, barely nodding, her grip on Rio’s shirt tightening for just a second.

“Okay,” Rio says quietly, pressing a gentle kiss to her forehead, careful not to shift her weight. “I love you.”

Agatha doesn’t open her eyes, but Rio feels the curve of her smile grow large against her skin.

“I love you too.”

2025

Saturday

The front door closes immediately behind her, the sound sharp and final, sealing Agatha out into the cold night air. The porch light hums faintly overhead, casting a dull glow that does nothing to warm her. She doesn’t take a step. Doesn’t move. For a moment, she forgets how to breathe.

Her chest tightens violently.

She brings a hand to her mouth, smothering the sound as a sob claws its way up her throat anyway. It slips out broken and quiet, like her body doesn’t quite know what to do with it. She leans back against the door, the wood cold even through her coat, grounding her just enough to keep her upright as silent sobs shake through her frame. Tears spill freely now, hot against her cheeks, blurring the porch light into useless streaks.

Why the fuck would she say that?

The words replay immediately, cruel and vivid, each one landing all over again. I don’t wanna see your face. She squeezes her eyes shut, jaw trembling.

It wasn’t fair.

It wasn’t true- not really.

She just pushed away the one person who still showed up for her. The one person who remembered things, who tried, who cared enough to hold on even when Agatha refused to acknowledge her.

And for what?

Some half-rotted grudge from twenty years ago? Anger? The fact that Rio refuses to disappear when Agatha tells her to?

No. It’s not that simple, not that easy, and she knows it.

It’s because loving Rio feels dangerous.

Because it feels like stepping back into something she never survived the first time.

She’d promised herself not to do that again. She won’t.

Her breathing stutters as another wave of tears breaks loose. The wind cuts through the porch, sharp and unforgiving, raising goosebumps along her arms. She shivers, hugging herself, finally about to pull away from the door-

When she hears it.

A sound from the other side.

Soft at first. Then unmistakable.

Crying.

No- sobbing.

Rio.

Agatha freezes. Her heart drops so hard it feels like it hits her stomach. Slowly, like she’s afraid the door might burn her, she turns back and presses her palm flat against the wood. She leans her forehead against the door, eyes squeezing shut as Rio’s broken, uneven breaths bleed through the barrier.

Each one feels like a knife.

She did this.

She made her cry like this.

Not a sharp argument cry, not an angry one- this is different. This is the sound of someone coming apart when they finally stop holding it together. Agatha’s throat tightens painfully, her own tears spilling faster now, guilt pooling thick and suffocating in her chest.

This is her fault.

She digs her nails into her palm, pinching hard like she can shock herself out of it, like pain might drown out the thoughts, the sound, the weight of everything she just shattered. It doesn’t work. Nothing does.

Finally, she pulls away from the door, forcing her legs to move before she can change her mind. She walks quickly- almost angrily- down the porch steps toward her car, heart constricting with every step away from the sound behind her.

It’s for the better, she tells herself desperately.

This is what needed to happen.

At least it’s over now.

The realization hits like a hollow echo, and fresh tears spill down her face as she reaches for the car door.

It’s over now.

Friday

“Honey, I’m heading out now,” Agatha calls to Ralph as she crosses the living room, grabbing her coat from the back of the couch.

Ralph doesn’t bother looking away from the TV. He just lifts a hand- lazy, absent, barely even a gesture- and grunts a low, distracted, “Bye.”

Agatha stands there for an extra second, waiting for… something. A glance. A word. A bare minimum acknowledgment. She gets none of it.

She exhales slowly, pressing her lips together before forcing herself to move again. She pulls the coat over her shoulders, adjusts the collar, and reaches for her keys on the entryway table. The metal jingles in her hand, the sound sharp in the quiet house.

“Okay then,” she mutters to no one, letting the door swing shut behind her a little too hard. The slam echoes through the porch, then dissolves into the evening air.

She walks to her car, each step heavier than the last, heels tapping against the driveway. By the time she sinks into the driver’s seat, the weight in her chest feels immovable.

She stares at the steering wheel for a long moment before turning the key, the engine coughing awake. Her fingers tap the leather, restless. She pulls out onto the street, the sky dimming into early dusk, and tries to focus on the road- on anything other than the silence she walked out of.

She doesn’t expect much from Ralph anymore. She hasn’t for a long time. But she’d thought-hoped- he’d at least say something today. A word. A gesture. A stupid little cupcake with a candle. Something.

Not having him even acknowledge her birthday all day shouldn’t sting this badly.

But it does.

It hits her low in the stomach, sharp and humiliating in a way she refuses to say out loud.

She drags in a breath, tight and shaky, and keeps driving.

At least she’ll get to be with Jen tonight. That’s what she keeps telling herself as she merges into the traffic. Jen always means well. She’s busy, overworked, juggling a new girlfriend and a job that practically eats her alive. Forgetting a birthday happens. It’s normal. It’s reasonable.

Agatha repeats that logic like a script she’s memorized.

She can’t be upset.

She can’t be disappointed.

It wouldn’t be fair.

Even if, deep down, there’s a tiny, stupid sting she refuses to acknowledge. A sting from no one in her life remembering her birthday.

She forces her shoulders back, trying to shrug it off. She should not be upset. She shouldn’t let this get to her. Adults don’t need celebrating or cake or confetti. Birthdays aren’t supposed to matter anymore.

She keeps her gaze fixed on the road, inhaling as the city whirs past her windows. At least she’s getting out of the house tonight. That’s something. She’ll get a meal with her friend. She won’t be sitting on the couch listening to Ralph breathe like a dying lawnmower while she tries not to cry into a microwave dinner.

It should be more than enough.

She tries to believe that.

Her eyes flick to the rearview mirror. The sparkly eyeshadow catches the fading sunlight, a dull shimmer across her eyelids. She put it on this morning with a silly hope- maybe Ralph would notice, maybe he’d ask if they were going out later, maybe he’d remember.

Didn’t happen.

She forces a small smile at herself in the mirror, lifting her brows the way she does when she’s trying to look lively. Her eyes crease at the corners.

She drags a finger beneath one eye, catching smudged mascara that had clumped in the humidity. It comes off on her fingertip, black and flaky. She stares at it like it’s a verdict- like it’s sentencing her to a life of old age and brittle bones and crusty skin.

Birthdays are a fucking bitch.

She pulls up outside Jen’s house and cuts the engine, sitting there for a moment with her hands resting uselessly in her lap. She takes a deep breath before climbing out of the car, bracing herself. She’d put on a black dress- purely for herself- but now, standing in the dim quiet of the street, she worries she might be overdressed. The thought makes her huff out a soft, humorless breath.

She tugs her khaki jacket tighter around her shoulders as a cool evening wind picks up, brushing against her bare legs. The walk up to the front door feels longer than it should. Too quiet. She glances at the windows and feels her stomach dip when she realizes all the lights are off.

Did she forget they made plans?

The thought settles heavy in her chest. She steps up to the door and knocks, then rocks back on her heels, waiting.

Nothing.

She knocks again, a little firmer this time, pulse thudding in her ears.

Still nothing.

Her hand hesitates before reaching for the handle. She presses down gently- and the door slides open.

“Jen?” she calls, peeking her head around the doorframe before stepping inside, suddenly hyper-aware of how loud her boots sound against the floor. She fumbles along the wall, fingers brushing paint and picture frames as she searches for the light.

Her fingertips catch the switch. She flicks it upward.

The room floods with bright light.

“Surprise!”

The shout crashes over her all at once. Confetti rains down through the air, glittering as it falls. Agatha jerks back with a startled gasp, heart slamming into her ribs. Jen stands directly across from her, laughing as she blows into a party horn, surrounded by familiar faces and decorations she somehow didn’t see coming.

“What!?” Agatha yells, shock giving way to a real, genuine smile. Her hand flies to her chest, trying to slow her racing heart. “Jen! You guys!”

Jen rushes toward her and throws her arms around Agatha in a tight hug, squeezing like she’s afraid to let go. When she pulls back, her hands stay planted on Agatha’s shoulders, eyes warm and shining.

“Happy birthday, Agatha,” she whispers with a smile.

“You remembered?” Agatha breathes, her smile bright as her eyes glass over.

“Bitch, of course I remembered!” Jen says immediately, grinning. “You really thought I’d forget your birthday?”

“Well, actually-” Agatha starts, barely getting the words out before she’s cut off by a sudden blur of movement.

“Happy birthday, queen!”

Billy barrels toward her at full speed and crashes straight into her, the impact almost knocking them both off balance. Agatha lets out a laugh at the nickname as he wraps her in a fierce hug, his face buried into her shoulder.

“Thank you, Billy,” she says fondly.

He pulls back, grinning wide and unapologetic. Warmth blooms in Agatha’s chest as she reaches up and ruffles his hair, deliberately messing up his curls before he scurries off.

She finally gets a second to breathe and looks around the room properly. Familiar faces smile back at her from every direction- Alice leaning against the counter with a drink already in hand, Yelena mid-laugh on the couch, eyes bright. Her friends. All of them. Here.

The tightness in her chest eases, just a little.

And then her eyes land on Rio.

Of course.

Rio stands near the edge of the room like she’s trying not to take up space, hands tucked into her pockets. When their eyes meet, Rio offers a small, soft smile- careful, almost tentative- before quickly looking away, like she’s worried she’s already crossed some invisible line.

Agatha swallows.

She forces herself to look elsewhere, taking in the rest of the room. Purple streamers drape across the walls and doorways, curling gently at the ends. Gold, glittering stars hang from the ceiling, catching the light as people move beneath them. Strings of twinkling lights wind along the banister and shelves, making the whole house glow softly, like something out of a dream.

It’s beautiful.

Everything she didn’t let herself hope for.

She turns back to Jen, disbelief written all over her face. “You did all this?” she asks, genuinely stunned.

Jen shrugs with a smug little smirk. “I had a little help.”

Agatha opens her mouth to press her- help from who?- but Jen is already slipping away toward the kitchen, weaving effortlessly through the crowd.

“Let’s get this party started!” Jen calls out.

Cheers erupt immediately, voices overlapping, laughter spilling into every corner of the house. The quiet heaviness Agatha carried with her all day dissolves into noise and warmth and life.

Agatha weaves through the crowd, offering smiles and brief hugs as she goes, stopping here and there to exchange a few words before moving on again. Everywhere she turns, someone is laughing, talking over music, reaching for her arm to say hello. It’s loud and warm and overwhelming in a way that feels almost unreal after the quiet of her day.

She eventually makes her way toward the kitchen, spotting Jen ahead of her. Jen has the fridge door open, already in full host mode, pulling bottles of champagne from the shelves and lining them up on the counter. Alice stands nearby, taking one and expertly twisting the cork free with a sharp pop.

Alice notices Agatha hovering in the doorway and grins.

“Let’s get the birthday girl a drink shall we?”

“Oh, god, yes please,” Agatha groans, relief washing over her. She moves instinctively toward Jen’s cabinet, muscle memory kicking in as she opens it and reaches for a stack of glasses, already knowing exactly where they’re kept.

Jen bats her hands away without looking. “You shouldn’t be doing that, you’re the guest of honor.”

“Jen, it’s fine, I don’t mind.”

“Nono. Sit.” Jen answers immediately, not leaving room for argument.

Alice laughs softly and steers Agatha toward one of the stools at the counter. Agatha allows herself to be guided, perching on the seat with a dramatic sigh, the tension in her shoulders finally loosening just a little.

Jen pours Agatha a generous glass of champagne, the bubbles fizzing loudly as they rise. She fills her own next, then Alice’s, sliding the glasses across the counter with practiced ease.

Jen lifts her glass. “To Agatha.”

“To Agatha,” Alice echoes.

They clink glasses, the sound bright and sharp, and Agatha takes a long sip. The champagne burns pleasantly down her throat, warm and grounding.

“I still can’t believe you guys did all this for me,” Agatha says, shaking her head slightly as she reaches for the bowl beside her. She grabs a handful of chips, the salty smell mixing with the sweetness of chocolate-covered strawberries and the richness of the charcuterie spread laid out across the counter.

“You deserve it, babe,” Jen replies easily as Agatha tosses a chip into her mouth and crunches down. “I know it’s been a stressful year.”

“You can say that again,” Agatha scoffs. She leans farther over the buffet, grabbing another snack without bothering to look, her eyes scanning the room as more guests begin to funnel into the kitchen, drawn by the drinks and the food. The space grows louder, warmer, filled with overlapping conversations and laughter.

Jen is quickly pulled into a discussion near the fridge, Alice moments later, leaving Agatha momentarily alone at the counter. She finishes her glass and stands up, reaching for the bottle, topping herself off without hesitation. She deserves at least that much.

She’s mid-pour when she feels it- that subtle shift in the air, the unmistakable awareness of someone standing too close.

Rio.

Agatha turns her head and finds her suddenly right there, their arms brushing lightly as Rio reaches past her. The contact is brief but electric, a cold ripple running straight down Agatha’s spine.

She studies Rio before she can stop herself. She looks… off. Exhausted. Her eyes are glassy and hollow, dark circles bruising the skin beneath them. Her cheekbones cut sharp against her face, making her look thinner, almost brittle.

“Sorry,” Rio murmurs, her voice low as she reaches above Agatha’s hand for the bottle. “Just getting a drink.”

“Oh- uh, yeah,” Agatha replies, distracted, her eyes flicking downward as Rio pours quickly. “No worries.”

Rio straightens with her glass and turns fully toward her, slipping on a soft smile that doesn’t quite reach her eyes. It looks practiced. Careful. “Happy birthday, Agatha,” she whispers.

Agatha feels something heavy settle in her chest. She returns the smile anyway, smaller, restrained. “Thanks.”

For just a second, Rio’s gaze lingers- like she wants to say something else, like there’s a comment sitting right behind her eyes. Then she nods once, sharp and final, and turns away, disappearing back into the noise of the party.

Agatha watches her go longer than she means to, champagne forgotten in her hand, the sweetness of the night dimming just slightly around the edges.

“Happy birthday to you!”

The crowd finishes the song, and Agatha pastes on a smile that feels a second too stiff, heat blooming across her cheeks. God, she hates sitting through that song. No one should be required to endure that torture. Ever.

Jen swoops in immediately with the knife, carving out an aggressively generous slice of red velvet and placing it into Agatha’s hands like an offering. Agatha murmurs a thank you as plates circulate through the room, guests already leaning toward the cake with impatience.

Her attention drifts- inevitably- to Rio.

Jen tries to hand her a plate, and Rio shakes her head with a polite, practiced smile. Jen hesitates, then shrugs and moves on. Something tightens in Agatha’s chest before she can stop herself, and she steps closer.

“You don’t want cake?” she asks lightly, taking a dramatic bite of her own and groaning at the taste.

“I’m good,” Rio says, stiff, lifting her glass instead.

“You love red velvet,” Agatha says- and only realizes too late that she’s said it at all.

Rio’s jaw tightens. “I used to.”

The words land harder than they should.

Agatha frowns. “What changed?”

“Nothing.” Rio’s tone is clipped now, her eyes sharpening as they flick up to meet Agatha’s.

That- of all things- makes Agatha irrationally upset. Her stomach twists, a sudden, inexplicable need rising in her chest. To fix it. To prove something. Maybe she’s just being petty. Maybe she hates being contradicted. Maybe it’s something else entirely.

“Just have a bite,” she says, already lifting her fork toward Rio’s mouth. “It’s good.”

Rio freezes, eyes flicking from Agatha’s face to the fork like she’s weighing a much bigger decision than cake warrants. The room seems to quiet around them, or maybe that’s just Agatha’s imagination.

Finally, reluctantly, Rio leans forward and takes the bite.

Agatha watches her chew, waiting.

“Well?” she asks, unable to hide the smug edge creeping into her voice.

Rio exhales through her nose, tongue pressing into her cheek. “It’s… pretty good,” she admits quietly.

Agatha’s chest loosens, satisfaction blooming before she can question it. “You want the rest?” she asks, already holding the plate out- automatic, instinctive, utterly absurd. It’s her birthday cake- but she keeps her hand outstretched towards Rio anyway.

Rio studies the plate like it might bite back, then shakes her head. “It’s okay,” she says, barely audible.

Agatha frowns. “We can share.”

Rio hesitates, fingers fidgeting at her side before she sighs. “Yeah. Okay.”

They trade bites, passing the fork back and forth without comment. The cake disappears faster than Agatha expects, crumbs and frosting gone before the moment can fully settle.

When it’s finished, Rio clears her throat, eyes dropping to the floor.

“So, I- um-” Agatha starts, grasping for something normal to say, something safe.

“I have to go to the bathroom,” Rio cuts in, already turning away.

She’s gone before Agatha can respond.

“Oh. Okay,” Agatha murmurs to no one in particular, staring after her with the empty plate still in her hands. She sets it down slowly, brow furrowed, trying to shake off the strange, unsettled feeling curling in her chest.

Is this because of what she said last week?

Can Rio not face her anymore?

Is she really done? For good?

She makes herself push the thoughts away. She reminds herself that she shouldn’t- doesn’t- care. Then she straightens, forces another smile, and lets the noise of the party close back in around her.

“Who’s ready for karaoke?” Jen shouts, voice already slurred with enthusiasm as she climbs onto the coffee table, grasping a tiny plastic microphone.

A wave of cheers rolls through the living room as people scramble for spots- piling onto the couch, sinking to the floor, perching on armrests, crowding around the TV eagerly.

“I’ve put together a full ABBA mix,” Jen announces proudly, dragging out every word before dramatically pointing at Agatha. “For the biggest fan I know.”

“Oh, please,” Agatha scoffs, wandering in with a champagne glass dangling loosely from her fingers. She drops onto the couch with a lazy sprawl. “I’m not that obsessed.”

Billy swivels on the armrest to face her. “Agatha, once you suspended me without pay because I hadn’t seen Mamma Mia.”

“Uh- so you’d have time to watch it,” she shoots back immediately, throwing her hands up like it’s the only obvious explanation. “Also, you’re literally gay. How had you not seen Mamma Mia?”

“Not the point,” Billy mutters.

Rio enters the room then, and Agatha feels it before she sees it- a brief hitch in her focus, a pull she pretends not to notice. Rio takes the far end of the couch beside her, leaving a deliberate gap between them, eyes forward, posture closed. The space feels louder than the music ever could. She catches a glimpse of her eyes- now red and glassy.

Was she crying?

Agatha forces her attention back to Billy. “Okay, fine. Whatever. I like ABBA,” she concedes dramatically. “Just play the stupid song.”

Jen physically bounces with joy as she smacks the shuffle button. The opening notes of “Angel Eyes” hit- and Agatha screams.

“Oh my god- yes!”

She’s on her feet instantly, abandoning her seat and nearly sloshing champagne over the rim of her glass as she sets it down somewhere vaguely safe. She grabs Jen’s hands and yanks her off the table. “Come on! Get over here with me! Let’s fucking go!”

From the corner of her eye, Agatha catches Billy leaning toward Rio, loudly whispering, “Is she drunk?”

Rio leans in just enough to answer, a small, sad smirk tugging at her mouth. “Maybe. But she’s also just fucking insane about ABBA.”

Billy snorts.

Agatha barely hears any of it. She’s already facing Jen, singing at the top of her lungs as the lyrics kick in, hands flying, hips swaying, joy loud and unfiltered.

“Sometimes when I'm lonely I sit and think about him

And it hurts to remember all the good times

When I thought I could never live without him”

Agatha’s grin falters before she can stop it. Just for a beat. The words land too close, too sharp, slipping straight past the buzz in her head and lodging somewhere painful in her chest. She keeps singing anyway, voice loud and theatrical.

“And I wonder does it have to be the same

Every time when I see him, will it bring back all the pain?

Ah-ha-ha, how can I forget that name?”

Her throat tightens. She swallows, forcing air into her lungs, forcing the smile back onto her face. This is supposed to be fun. This is supposed to be stupid and campy and drunk and easy. Not… this.

She can feel it before she sees it- the weight of someone’s attention pressing into her skin. Her eyes betray her, sliding sideways despite her best efforts. They land on Rio. Rio isn’t smiling. She isn’t laughing with the rest of them. She’s just watching- eyes wide, unblinking, dark with something unreadable. Hurt. Longing. Guilt. Maybe all of it tangled together. It’s too much. It always has been.

Those fucking eyes.

“Look into his angel eyes

One look and you're hypnotized

He'll take your heart and you must pay the price”

She turns sharply back to Jen, grabbing her hands, spinning her like this is nothing more than a ridiculous karaoke bit. Jen’s laughing, head thrown back, completely unaware that Agatha’s heart is trying to claw its way out of her chest.

“Look into his angel eyes”

She refuses to look back. Refuses to give Rio that. She fixes her gaze on the TV, on the bouncing lyrics, on anything that isn’t the woman sitting a few feet away who still has the power to undo her with a glance.

When the song finally ends, Agatha laughs breathlessly, tossing her hands in the air as the room erupts in cheers. She grabs her glass and collapses back onto the couch like she’s just run a mile.

Relief washes through her- thin, fragile, temporary.

She doesn’t look at Rio again.

“Okay, who’s up next?” Jen yells, cutting cleanly through the quiet that settles after the song. She pivots, zeroing in on Alice, who’s sitting cross-legged on the floor. “Alice?”

“Oh, absolutely not.” Alice answers instantly, shaking her head and shooting Jen a warning look.

Jen registers the glare and immediately redirects. “Rio?”

Rio scoffs from the couch. “I’m good. Thanks.”

“Oh, come on! Don’t be such a downer!” Jen sing-songs, clapping her hands together. “Ri-o, Ri-o, R-io!” She starts chanting, waving her arms until a few people join in just to shut her up.

Rio exhales, defeated. “Fine.” She pushes herself up from the couch and walks toward the front of the living room just as Jen squeals and hits play.

Andante, Andante flashes onto the screen.

Agatha’s stomach drops.

Fucking hell.

She watches the expression flicker across Rio’s face- surprise first, then hesitation, then something like quiet panic- as her eyes dart briefly to Agatha before looking away again.

“Can we choose a different one?” Rio asks softly, almost under her breath.

Jen looks personally wounded. “No can do, sweetie,” she says, crossing her arms. “The playlist has spoken.”

Rio sighs and looks back at Agatha. This time she doesn’t look away quickly. She just… waits. Like she’s asking permission. Like she doesn’t know how to stand up there without it.

Agatha swallows, then gives the smallest nod. It’s fine. Just sing.

The music swells, and Jen presses the tiny microphone into Rio’s hand. Rio’s grip is shaky as she lifts it, her voice quiet but steadying as she begins.

“Take it easy with me, please

Touch me gently like a summer evening breeze

Take your time, make it slow

Andante, Andante

Just let the feeling grow”

Agatha can’t look away. Rio stands alone in front of everyone, shoulders slightly hunched, like she’s bracing for impact. She looks fragile in a way that hurts- careful, exposed, like one wrong glance could shatter her. She avoids Agatha’s eyes completely, scanning the room instead, searching for something solid to anchor herself to.

Agatha exhales slowly. Then, without thinking too hard about it, she pulls out her phone and turns on the flashlight. She raises it overhead, waving it gently from side to side. She shoots Billy a sharp look- help me out- and he immediately follows suit. One by one, more lights flick on, until the room is glowing softly, warm and steady.

Rio notices.

Her shoulders loosen. A small, genuine smile tugs at her lips as she keeps singing.

“I'm your music, I'm your song

Play me time and time again and make me strong

Make me sing, make me sound

Andante, Andante

Tread lightly on my ground

Andante, Andante

Oh please, don't let me down”

Agatha keeps her arm moving in a slow, steady arc, the flashlight swaying back and forth in time with the music. She focuses on the motion- on the weight of the phone in her hand, on the dull ache in her shoulder- anything but the memories threatening to surface. If she lets herself think about Nicky, about Rio, about late nights and shared dorm rooms and a time when her life felt full and possible, she knows she’ll fall apart.

And she cannot fall apart.

Not here. Not now.

Her jaw tightens as she bites the inside of her cheek, hard enough to ground herself, until the metallic tang of blood floods her mouth. She drops her gaze to the floor, refusing to look at Rio, refusing to see the way her voice curls around the lyrics like a confession meant only for her.

The song stretches on, every second dragging, until finally- mercifully- it ends. Applause erupts around the room, but Agatha barely hears it. She exhales a breath she didn’t realize she’d been holding.

She shifts along the couch, leaving space beside her where Rio was stationed minutes before. An offering. A silent you can sit here.

Rio doesn’t take it.

Instead, she murmurs something too quiet to hear and slips past the coffee table, moving quickly, head down, already halfway out of the room before Agatha can even process it.

“Um- okay,” Jen says awkwardly, watching her go. “Anyway… who’s next?”

Jen’s voice fades into the background. Agatha doesn’t register who gets volunteered next, doesn’t register the laughter or the music restarting. Her eyes are locked on the empty space where Rio had been sitting, on the faint impression she left behind.

Her chest sinks.

She hates the instinctive worry that flares in her gut. Hates that she wonders if Rio’s okay. Hates that she still cares.

A small, treacherous part of her urges her to stand up. To follow. To check on her. Just to make sure she’s alright.

Agatha crushes that thought immediately.

It’s not her responsibility.

It’s not her place.

It shouldn’t matter- it doesn’t matter.

She forces herself to turn back toward the TV, toward the lights and the noise and the next ABBA song already starting up. She raises her glass, takes a long drink, and lets the music swallow her whole.

Anything to drown out the thoughts.

Anything to keep herself from feeling the empty space Rio left behind.

Alice grabs Agatha by the hand and tugs her toward the dining room before she can protest. The table is overtaken by gifts- bags, boxes, tissue paper spilling over the edges- and Agatha lets out a disbelieving sigh.

“Oh my god,” she mutters. “You people are ridiculous.”

“Okay! Time for gifts!” Alice announces loudly, clapping her hands once. She hesitates. “…I think.”

Agatha shoots her a look. “You think? Where’s Jen?”

Alice grins, tilting her head. “Jen is currently on a brief recess to… recuperate.”

Billy pokes his head around the doorframe. “She’s drunk as fuck,” he supplies helpfully, already stepping into the room. The other guests follow, engrossed in a multitude of side conversations as they filter in.

“Yeah,” Alice says, nodding. “So I’m temporary host!” She throws her arms up with exaggerated cheer.

Agatha groans softly. “God help us all,” she mutters, earning herself a light smack on the arm.

“Hey,” Alice says, laughing. “Respect the host.”

Before Agatha can say anything else, Billy lunges forward, snatching a gift off the table and shoving it into her arms. “Do mine first, Agatha!”

Agatha huffs, adjusting her grip on the box as she eyes him. “This better be good for how fucking eager you are.” She mumbles, tearing off the wrapping paper. Inside the box sits a brown, shiny striped stone.

Agatha looks up at Billy, raising an eyebrow. “You got me a rock?”

“It’s a tiger’s eye,” he explains. “It’s supposed to help you channel your personal power and inner strength- and provide protection and confidence and whatnot. I figured you could put it on your desk at work, so everyone knows you’re a badass boss. Or just- anywhere you might want a little extra power, I don’t know.” He shrugs, grinning.

Agatha’s face breaks into a genuine smile as she picks up the stone, letting her fingers trace the smooth stripes.

“Thank you, Billy,” she says softly. “I love it.”

Billy’s grin widens, practically glowing with excitement that she actually liked his gift.

Her other friends finish handing over their gifts- a colorful mix of gift cards, scented candles, bath bombs, and chocolate. Agatha opens each one with a bright smile, letting herself be swept up in their thoughtfulness. Every card, every little trinket, makes her finally feel noticed, cared for- for the first time in a while. Slowly, the stack of gifts dwindles down, until only one small bag remains.

“Who’s this from?” she asks, lifting it toward herself. The room falls quiet, all eyes darting around for answers. Silence.

“It must be from Rio,” Billy offers with a shrug.

Agatha freezes. Her gaze flicks across the room. “Where is she?”

“She left a little while ago,” Alice answers, her eyes studying Agatha, confused as to how she didn’t know.

“She did?” Agatha asks, her voice tight. Her smile falters as her chest sinks slightly. Alice nods, and Agatha exhales softly, forcing herself to readjust, to plaster the practiced smile back onto her face. But inside, her stomach twists, a hollow weight she can’t shake.

With a slow hand, she pulls the bag closer and peels back the tissue paper, her fingers trembling just a little. Beneath the wrapping, there’s a small, flat box. She lifts the lid, revealing a delicate locket inside.

It’s dark purple, almost plum, with three tiny white figures etched across the front, framed by a thin, intricate gold edge. Her fingers hover over it, brushing against the smooth metal. She flicks the locket open.

Inside rests a small, perfectly pressed daisy, encased in clear resin. Her breath catches.

Nicky’s birth flower.

Well- what should’ve been his birth flower.

Her eyes gloss over, and for a moment the chatter, the clinking glasses, the laughter around her- all of it- falls away. Her chest tightens, and she brings the locket close to her heart, pressing it gently against her chest. The weight of it, of the thought behind it, squeezes at her ribs. Her fingers trace the smooth edges of the locket, careful not to disturb the pressed daisy inside.

Finally, she closes it slowly, exhaling a breath. The warmth of the metal seeps into her palm, grounding her just enough. She swallows hard, her lips curving into a small, shaky smile.

She undoes the clasp of the chain, sliding the locket around her neck until it rests perfectly in the center of her chest, right in line with her heart. She presses her fingers to it, tracing the edge, feeling the cool metal beneath her touch.

The room hums around her- guests drifting in and out, some grabbing last-minute snacks, most murmuring goodbyes as they filter toward the door. Their voices feel distant.

Agatha drifts toward the living room, her steps unsteady, drawn by the faint glow of her phone screen lying on the table. She picks it up, fingers trembling as she opens the thread with Rio. Her thumbs hover over the keyboard for a second too long before she types-

9:21 Thank you for the necklace. I love it.

Her heart feels impossibly heavy as she hits send, each beat hammering in her chest. Rio reads it instantly. Agatha stares at the screen, waiting.

A minute passes.

Two.

Nothing.

Just Read 9:21.

She exhales slowly, trying to steady herself, but the air feels too thin. Her chest aches as if the silence is pressing down on her from all sides. She sets the phone down, rocking slightly on her heels, wishing the room would quiet, wishing the night could pause- just for a moment.

It’s fine.

She’s fine.

She repeats it over and over, the words a brittle mantra she barely believes anymore. But even as she says it, the truth seeps in. Her chest still tightens every time she glances at her phone. Her stomach knots with a mix of longing and dread, and for a moment she wonders if she’s imagining the weight she feels, the tiny pull in her chest that won’t let her shrug this off.

Her eyes drift over the empty space where Rio had been earlier, heart twinging at the memory of her reserved smile, the careful way she’d sat down as far away as possible. She hates herself for thinking about it. She hates herself for wanting to check if Rio’s okay. She hates that she even cares. But she can’t shake the thought, the silent whisper of guilt tangled inside her.

Agatha closes her eyes, letting out a shaky breath. The room keeps moving around her, voices rising and falling, laughter echoing faintly from the kitchen. Her head throbs. She bites at her cuticles, a nervous tic that leaves a faint metallic taste in her mouth.

It’s okay.

Rio’s leaving her alone.

She’s finally moving on, just like she’d asked.

This is what she wanted.

…

Right?

 

Chapter End Notes

I really went into this with the intention of writing a happy chapter.

Chapter 9: Chapter Nine

Chapter Notes

TW: disordered eating, child loss

See the end of the chapter for more notes

2005

Agatha wakes to the cold, unmistakable feeling of something damp between her legs.

For a moment, she lies still, suspended in the haze between sleep and waking, hoping it’s nothing- hoping it’s her imagination, sweat, anything but what her brain already knows. Her heart begins to thud harder as she shifts slightly beneath the covers. The sheet clings to her skin.

She swallows, throat tight, and reaches down with trembling fingers. When she pulls her hand back, it’s dark in the room, but she can still just make out the crimson covering her fingertips. Her stomach drops, fear blooming sharp and fast in her chest.

“Rio,” she whispers, turning toward her. Her hands find Rio’s shoulders, shaking her gently at first, then harder when she doesn’t wake. “Rio.”

Rio groans and rolls onto her side, still wrapped in sleep. “Agatha?” she mumbles. “What’s wrong?”

Agatha pulls her hands back into her lap, fingers curling into the sheets like she might tear through them. Her voice feels fragile when she speaks again, quieter than she expects. “I-” She stops, breath catching. “I think I’m bleeding.”

The silence that follows feels deafening.

Rio jolts upright, the covers ripped away in a rush of movement, and Agatha flinches slightly. She watches Rio’s eyes track downward, sees the exact moment her expression changes- shock giving way to panic.

“Oh my god.” Rio says. “Fuck.”

Agatha follows her gaze to the mattress. The red looks impossibly dark against the pale sheets, spreading beneath her in a wide stain. Her chest tightens, breath coming shallow now. She can’t make herself move.

Rio is out of bed in seconds, swearing under her breath, pacing once nervously before snapping into action. “I’m calling an ambulance. I’m calling right now.”

Agatha stays where she is, perched on the edge of the bed, hands clenched so tightly in the sheets her knuckles ache. The pain from her stomach is unbearable, and the room feels suddenly too large, too quiet, the hum of Rio’s movement distant and unreal. Her vision blurs at the edges.

She hears the faint clicks of a phone being dialed. 911.

Rio looks back at her then, fear written plainly across her face, and something in Agatha’s chest twists painfully. “Sweetheart, you okay?” Rio asks, trying- and failing- to keep her voice steady.

Agatha wants to say yes. She wants to reassure her, to be the calm one, to make this less terrifying than it feels. Instead, she can only nod slowly, eyes fixed on the floor, glassy and unfocused, as the ringing cuts off and a muffled voice bleeds through the phone.

Rio starts pacing again. Agatha sees her- sees her mouth moving, her hands flying as she speaks- but none of it reaches her. The sound drains away, replaced by a high, piercing ring that fills her head, drowning out everything else.

Rio lowers the phone. Crosses the room to Agatha. Says something.

Agatha doesn’t hear it. She doesn’t respond. The world feels thick and distant, like she’s trapped underwater. All she can focus on is Rio’s face, the fear carved into it, the way her eyes shine with unshed tears. Rio’s hands close around Agatha’s arms, grounding and warm, her grip just tight enough to anchor her there.

Time stretches, slow and cruel. Seconds drag into something heavier, longer, until Agatha can’t tell how much has passed.

Then- footsteps in the hallway. Voices. A sharp knock cutting through the air.

“Paramedics.”

The door opens, and suddenly the room is crowded with motion- dark uniforms, clipped voices, the rustle of equipment. Someone asks questions. Someone else kneels in front of her. A light flashes briefly in her eyes.

She doesn’t answer. She doesn’t hear what they’re saying.

She watches Rio step forward, mouth moving rapidly as the paramedics nod. One of them reappears with a stretcher, metal unfolding with a sharp, efficient clatter.

Hands guide Agatha up from the bed. The sheets cling to her, heavy with blood, reluctant as they peel her free before easing her back down. The mattress disappears beneath her, replaced by vinyl and straps and the too-bright ceiling lights.

Rio walks alongside her, gripping her hand so tightly it almost hurts, as if afraid she might slip away. Agatha’s eyelids grow unbearably heavy, her thoughts turning soft and fuzzy, the world narrowing to the squeeze of Rio’s hand as everything else fades.

Agatha wakes to the steady beep of machines and the low murmur of voices somewhere far away, the sounds drifting in and out of her consciousness. She doesn’t open her eyes. Her body feels heavy, unresponsive, wrapped in layers of scratchy cotton. For a few seconds, she floats there, suspended between sleep and waking- until a sharp, burning ache flares across her stomach, ripping a quiet, broken sound from her throat.

She stirs weakly, fingers twitching against the sheets, trying to pull air deep into her lungs. Her mouth opens as she tries to speak, to ask where she is, what happened- but all that comes out is a rough, fractured grunt that barely sounds human.

“Agatha?”

The voice cuts through the haze. It’s close. Fractured. Familiar.

Rio.

Awareness trickles back slowly. Agatha becomes conscious of a hand wrapped around hers- warm, steady, grounding- resting against the cold firmness of the mattress beneath her back. She forces her eyelids open, blinking sluggishly as the light burns. Her vision swims, blurring objects together, until the room slowly takes shape. Pale walls, humming machines, and wires snaking toward her body. Blinking monitors flash rhythmically beside her.

Then her eyes find Rio.

She looks like she hasn’t slept. Her hair is tangled, pulled back harshly, and dark circles lay deep beneath red, swollen eyes. Her face is pale, streaked faintly with dried tears, like she’s been crying for hours without stopping long enough to breathe.

“Agatha?” Rio whispers again, managing a small, fragile smile that trembles at the edges. “How are you feeling?”

Agatha swallows, her throat raw, her body aching in too many different ways. “My stomach hurts,” she murmurs, her voice hoarse and unfamiliar, as if it belongs to someone else. She draws in a shallow breath, unease curling tight in her chest. “A lot.”

The silence stretches, thick and wrong, her mouth not daring to form the question her brain is begging to ask.

A pause.

“How’s the baby?”

Rio doesn’t answer. The change is immediate- her shoulders stiffen, her jaw tightening as her tongue presses into her cheek. She looks down, away from Agatha, as if the floor might offer refuge from whatever she’s about to say.

“Rio?” Agatha asks softly, dread sinking deep and cold into her chest, too heavy. “Is Nicky okay?”

The name barely makes it past her lips.

Rio inhales shakily. When she looks back up, her eyes are glassy, tears spilling over despite her effort to hold them back. “The doctor said you had a placental abruption,” she says, voice trembling, words carefully thought out. “It cut off Nicky’s oxygen.”

Agatha feels each word land separately, slow and brutal, like something was breaking inside her piece by piece.

“They tried,” Rio continues, her voice cracking now, grief pouring through the words. “They were going to do a C-section, but it was too late.” Her breath stutters. “He didn’t make it.”

She tightens her grip on Agatha’s hand, clinging, tears falling freely now. “I’m so sorry, Agatha.”

The machines keep beeping. Steady. Indifferent. As if her whole world hadn’t just been ripped apart.

The room feels too quiet, too still, as the weight of it settles in- pressing down on Agatha’s chest until breathing feels impossible.

Agatha shakes her head slowly, weakly. “No,” she whispers, the word splintering as it leaves her mouth. “No, it can’t be.”

Tears spill down her cheeks as she squeezes her eyes shut. Her chest aches with every shallow breath. “I want more time,” she sobs. “Please. I want more time.”

She looks at Rio then- pleading, desperate- as if Rio could undo this, as if she could bend the laws of nature, as if she could rewind time and reach back and pull Nicky into their arms.

She can’t.

“I’m sorry,” Rio whispers, her voice barely there as she tightens her grip on Agatha’s hand. She drags in a shaky breath, wiping her wet cheeks with the back of her hand. “Agatha… there’s something else-”

A sharp knock cuts her off.

Agatha flinches at the sound as the hospital room door cracks open. An older woman steps inside, tall and rigid, long gray hair pulled back tightly from a face that looks carved from stone. Her eyes are sunken, her posture stiff, her expression unreadable and severe under the harsh fluorescent light.

Agatha’s heart drops into her stomach.

“Hello, Agatha,” the woman says, her voice flat, stripped of warmth or any softness.

Agatha swallows hard, her pulse spiking painfully. “Mom?” Her voice breaks around the word. “What are you doing here?”

2025

Saturday

Rio yanks the ribbon through the air, pulling it tight across the path before looping it around the trunk of a nearby tree. Her fingers fumble for a second, slick with sweat, before she knots it securely. She tears it free from the roll with her teeth, the sharp rip loud in the morning air, then steps back to check her work.

The finish sign stands just in front of the ribbon, planted firmly in the grass, bright and unmistakable- marking the end point for the runners.

The park is already crowded. Hundreds of people mill about, stretching, laughing, pacing with nervous energy. Runners cluster near the starting line, numbers pinned to their shirts, while spectators line the paths with coffee cups and phones in hand. Volunteers dart between them in matching shirts, clipboards tucked under their arms.

Rio’s stomach twists as she takes it all in.

So many people. So many possible donors.

They’re hoping to raise a few thousand dollars from today’s charity run, money they desperately need to keep the organization going strong the rest of the year, to fund programs and cleanups and everything they keep promising to do every week. She exhales slowly, forcing the tightness out of her lungs, then gathers her hair and twists it up into a ponytail.

With one last glance at the finish line, Rio turns and jogs back toward the starting area. More runners are arriving now, bouncing on their toes, rolling out calves, checking their watches. The low hum of anticipation grows louder by the second.

This is it, she thinks, weaving through the crowd, clipboard clutched tight in her hand. Everything is set.

She turns slowly, scanning the park, taking inventory the way she always does when she’s anxious. Wanda is near the starting line, crouched behind a runner, carefully pinning numbers to the backs of shirts with practiced efficiency. Alice stands a few feet away with a clipboard, checking people in as they arrive, calling out names and waving runners forward. Yelena weaves through the crowd, bright and energetic, pressing fliers into willing hands, already talking up the cause.

Then her gaze drifts farther out.

Agatha is standing on the grass not too far away, arms wrapped around herself, shoulders hunched inward like she’s trying to make herself smaller. She’s leaning close to Jen, speaking in a hushed, urgent tone, one hand twisting nervously in the fabric of her sleeve. Even from this distance, Rio can see the tension in her face- the tight jaw, the furrowed brow, the anxiety written plainly across her features.

Jen nods as Agatha speaks, listening intently. When Agatha finally stops, Jen doesn’t hesitate. She pulls her into a firm, grounding hug, one hand rubbing slow, comforting circles into her back. Agatha doesn’t pull away. She sinks into it.

Rio’s heart constricts painfully.

She doesn’t need to be close to know something’s wrong. She knows Agatha too well for that. The way she folds in on herself. The way she seeks quiet reassurance instead of asking for it outright. Whatever it is, it’s heavy enough to break through the distraction of the day.

Instinct flares sharp and immediate.

Go to her. Ask if she’s okay. Just stand nearby. Anything.

Her feet almost move before her brain catches up.

She forces herself to stop. To breathe. To remember.

Agatha made it clear- painfully clear- that she doesn’t want anything to do with Rio anymore. Doesn’t want her checking in, hovering, pretending things can still be fixed. Not after everything.

So Rio swallows hard and looks away.

She turns back toward the crowd, toward the noise and the responsibility waiting for her, shoving the image of Agatha trembling in Jen’s arms deep into the back of her mind.

It hurts. God, it hurts.

But she lets it. She accepts it.

She turns instead toward Billy, who’s standing off to the side of the path with no real purpose, hands shoved into his pockets as he watches the runners warm up. Rio exhales and walks over, grateful for the distraction.

“Hey, you running today?” she asks, dropping into a stretch and rolling her shoulders as she loosens up.

Billy snaps his head toward her. “Oh, I don’t run,” he answers quickly, laughing at the idea.

Rio raises an eyebrow, a small smile tugging at her mouth as she shakes her head.

“Are you running?” he adds, gesturing vaguely toward the course.

She flicks her gaze up at him, face flat with disbelief. “No, I’m mid-lunge right now just for fun,” she says dryly.

Billy smirks, cheeks flushing. “Right. Duh.”

Rio laughs, the sound coming easier than it has all morning, and switches legs, muscles pulling pleasantly as she stretches. For a brief second, the tension in her chest loosens.

“Rio.”

The sound of her name from behind her hits her hard. Her stomach sinks. Her heart stutters.

Shit.

She straightens slowly, turning her expression into something neutral- something safe- before turning around. She pastes on a smile that feels a little too tight around the edges.

“Agatha,” she says.

Agatha stands a few feet away, hands tucked into the pockets of tight navy sweats that cling to her legs. Rio’s gaze drops despite herself, heat rushing to her cheeks as she forces her eyes back up.

“You running today?”

“Yeah, I think so,” Agatha says softly. She hesitates, drawing in a breath like she’s bracing herself. “Why didn’t you answer my text yesterday?”

Rio’s breath catches, sharp and sudden. The question isn’t angry. It’s not accusing. It’s gentle. Almost tentative. That somehow makes it worse.

“I- I was busy. Sorry,” Rio says, the lie slipping out easier than the truth ever could.

Agatha watches her for a moment, eyes searching, her brow creasing slightly. The silence stretches, thin and uncomfortable.

“Oh, okay. Yeah, no worries,” she finally says with a small sigh. “I just wanted to say thank you. That’s all.”

Rio’s chest tightens. She offers a soft smile that doesn’t quite reach her eyes. “Oh yeah- it was nothing,” she replies, shrugging like it doesn’t matter, like it didn’t cost her sleep, or restraint, or every ounce of self-control she has.

For just a second, Agatha’s expression falters. It’s subtle- easy to miss- but Rio sees it. The flicker of something hurt before the mask slides back into place.

Agatha nods once, turns, and walks away without looking back.

Rio stays where she is, staring after her until she’s gone, then finally lets out a breath - slow and shaky.

She checks her phone. Five minutes until the race starts.

Her thumb hovers uselessly over the screen before she locks it again and slips it back into her pocket. She glances across the path toward Wanda, who’s stationed near a folding table, cheerfully handing out protein bars to any runner who wanders close enough. Wanda tears one open for herself, already halfway through it, unfazed by the chaos around her.

Rio considers going over- considers the quick hit of sugar, the excuse to delay what’s coming- but the thought fizzles out almost immediately. She exhales through her nose, pushes the impulse aside, and turns away, marching toward the starting line instead.

Her legs feel heavy. Her chest feels worse.

She slows as she reaches the cluster of runners, rolling her shoulders back, forcing herself to stand tall. Suddenly, the idea of running a 5K feels unbearable. She has nothing left to give- no energy, no focus, no emotional bandwidth to spare. Everything inside her feels scraped raw.

The rest of the runners gather beside her, filling in the space, shoes shuffling against the pavement, nervous laughter bubbling up around them. Out of the corner of her eye, she catches sight of Agatha.

Her stomach twists.

Rio drops her gaze immediately, fixing her attention on the ground, on her shoes, on the cracks in the pavement. Anything but Agatha. Anything but the way her presence pulls at her like gravity.

Alice steps into the center of the path, lifting the megaphone as the crowd settles along the edges, spectators lining the route with phones and coffee cups raised.

“Thank you everyone for showing up today,” Alice projects, her voice cutting cleanly through the noise. “Just a reminder that this is a charity run for Coven for Climate. So if you’d like to make a donation to help save our planet, you can go see Yelena over here and she’ll assist you,” she gestures toward Yelena, who beams and waves enthusiastically from a few feet away.

Alice steps off the path, moving to the side as she slips the megaphone down and reaches for the whistle hanging around her neck. “Now, is everybody ready?”

The runners erupt into cheers, the sound swelling and echoing through the park. Rio swallows hard, planting her feet, bracing herself.

“3… 2… 1…”

The whistle shrieks sharp and loud, slicing through the air. The group jolts forward all at once, feet pounding against the pavement as the crowd propels into a jog- and Rio is carried with them, heart hammering, running whether she’s ready or not.

Two miles into the race, Rio’s lungs burn like they’re on fire. Every breath is a conscious, painful effort, sharp and shallow, and each step sends a dull ache radiating through her legs, settling deep in her muscles.

She curses herself silently.

She used to be good at this. She used to run a 5K without thinking twice, without her body fighting her every step of the way. Running used to feel natural- freeing, even.

So why is she struggling now?

The question haunts her as she forces herself forward. She can’t give in to the pain. Can’t slow down. Can’t let herself stop.

She clenches her jaw and keeps moving, pushing past the burn, past the exhaustion, refusing to listen to the part of her body begging her to quit. She won’t.

Not long after, her vision begins to blur, the edges of the world fading together as dizziness courses through her head. The ground sways beneath her feet, the path tilting as her body starts to shut down. Her muscles weaken all at once, turning to jelly, barely responding when she tells them to move.

Her pace falters. Each step feels smaller than the last, her legs shaking as they struggle to carry her forward.

Keep going.

She clings to the thought like a lifeline.

You can do this. Just keep going.

Darkness creeps in from the edges of her vision, tunneling inward as she pants, her head spinning violently now. A faint ringing sound develops in her ears. Her instincts finally kick in, sharp and urgent.

She slows, the jog dissolving into a staggered walk, arms pumping uselessly as she fights to stay upright. She scans the path desperately, searching for help- anyone- along the sidelines.

Her gaze locks onto Billy, standing near the edge of the course, watching the runners pass. Relief flares, brief and desperate. She veers toward him, cutting across the grass, forcing air into her lungs.

“Billy,” she manages, the word barely audible.

He hears it. Spins toward her just in time.

Rio stumbles forward and collapses straight into his arms.

Her vision crawls back in slowly. The ground feels too close, the air too thin. She sways, heart hammering, and forces herself upright, embarrassment flushing hot through her chest. 

Fuck. Please not here. Not now.

“Rio?” Billy asks, his voice cutting through the ring in her head. “Are you okay?”

“I’m sorry,” she blurts, shame rushing in fast and sharp. “I’m sorry, I’m good.” The words tumble out on instinct. She presses her hands down on his forearms for balance, hating how much she needs the support.

“Rio, you almost fainted,” he says. “What happened?”

Her head still spins faintly, a low hum under her skull, but she forces a smile anyway. “I’m just tired,” she insists. “I’m okay. Really.” 

Please let that be enough. Please don’t make this a big thing.

Billy’s eyes flick over her face, her hands, the way she can’t quite stop trembling. His concern only makes the tight knot in her chest worse. “Rio, you’re so pale. I think we should go to the medical tent.”

“Oh, don’t be dramatic. I’m fine, I promise.” The lie tastes thin in her mouth. She says it anyway.

“You’re literally shaking,” he says flatly. “Come on. It’s only a few feet away. Let’s go.”

She opens her mouth to argue, but the effort feels too much. The thought of standing here any longer, of runners passing and staring, is unbearable. So when he gently steers her forward, she lets him. Fighting takes more energy than she has.

The medical tent looms ahead, white and impossibly exposed. Of course this is happening today, she thinks bitterly. Of course it is.

She hesitantly steps through the entrance. “Hey, Lilia,” Rio says, forcing the smallest smile she can manage.

Lilia turns, gray curls bouncing, and the second their eyes meet, Rio’s racing heartbeat calms.

Lilia has been the team’s standby medic since the organization began- seventeen years of scraped knees, heat exhaustion, dizziness spells, faintings. Seventeen years of knowing when Rio is lying before she even opens her mouth.

The woman’s basically family now.

“Rio,” Lilia says. “You look terrible. What’s wrong?”

She gestures to the cot, already reaching out, pressing the back of her hand to Rio’s forehead. The touch is gentle, automatic- and it makes Rio want to recoil. “Flu?”

Rio shakes her head quickly.

“She almost fainted while running,” Billy offers from the tent entrance.

Traitor, Rio thinks weakly, though she knows he’s right.

Lilia straightens, crossing her arms slowly. “Rio Vidal.”

The sound of her full name lands like a warning. Rio swallows hard, her throat suddenly dry.

“Have you eaten?”

Her gaze drops to the floor immediately. She presses her tongue into her cheek, buying time she doesn’t have. Lilia knows. She always knows. And Rio hates herself a little for being this predictable.

“And don’t even think about bullshitting me.”

“No,” Rio admits, the word barely making it past her lips.

It feels like failure, saying it out loud. It feels like being caught.

Lilia sighs, the sound soft but heavy, and looks at Rio with sad, sorrowful eyes.

Rio hates that look more than anything. She wishes Lilia would yell. Scold her. Call her reckless or irresponsible or stupid. Anger would be easier to swallow than pity. Pity feels like being seen too clearly.

She tears her gaze away, fixing it on her hands instead- her fingers curled tight in her lap, knuckles pale. Anywhere but Lilia’s face. She can feel Billy’s eyes on her from across the tent, watching quietly, thinking too much, saying nothing. The silence presses in.

“Let’s get some food in you then,” Lilia says.

Rio stiffens internally. Here it comes.

Lilia marches over to her purse, already unzipping it, silently pulling out a granola bar. She peels the wrapper back and holds it out. Rio hesitates before taking it, fingers closing reluctantly around the bar like it might hurt her.

There’s no point in arguing. Rio learned that lesson years ago. Lilia will not let her leave this tent until she eats, and Rio doesn’t have the strength- physical or emotional- to fight her today.

She takes a small, slow bite. The texture feels wrong in her mouth, too solid, too present. She chews like it hurts, jaw tight, throat working around every swallow.

Lilia turns away deliberately, busying herself with supplies, checking something on the table- anything to give Rio privacy. She knows. She always knows that being watched would only make this harder.

Rio lets her mind drift, forces it anywhere but the food. Anywhere but the calorie number she can already feel tallying itself up in the back of her head. She focuses on the noise outside the tent- the distant yelling, the cheers swelling and fading as runners pass.

They’re probably close to the end by now.

The thought twists something sharp in her chest.

She finishes the granola bar anyway, swallowing the last bite with effort, wiping her hands on her shorts as she looks over at Lilia expectantly.

Lilia doesn’t say anything. She just hands Rio a water bottle filled with brightly colored liquid.

“Drink,” she says. “Electrolytes.”

Rio shoots her a stubborn glare before lifting the bottle and taking a few gulps, the liquid too sweet on her tongue. She swallows hard and lowers it. “Can I go now?” she asks. “I need to finish the race.”

“Are you crazy?” Billy cuts in. “You’re gonna run again?”

Rio nods, jaw set. “I have to finish.”

Billy looks to Lilia immediately, waiting for her to shut this down, to be the bad guy.

Lilia only sighs again, rubbing a hand over her face before meeting Rio’s eyes. “Rio, I can’t legally stop you. But I highlyrecommend you don’t go back out there.”

Rio smirks, the expression sharp and defensive. “Yeah, I know. I’ll be fine.”

She leans in and plants a quick kiss on Lilia’s cheek before turning toward the entrance. “Thanks, Lilia,” she adds, already pushing through the tent flap.

The fresh air hits her like a slap.

As she steps back onto the path, guilt floods in fast and unforgiving. She hates herself for going into that tent. For letting herself be seen. For eating. For feeling like she’s ruined something she’d been building- some mental game only she was keeping score of.

But the race is still going. If she runs hard enough, fast enough, maybe it’ll make up for the calories she just consumed- the progress she just lost.

She hops back and forth on her feet, sharp and restless, like if she stops moving for even a second her body will realize how bad this actually feels. Then she takes off again, launching herself into a run faster than before- too fast, probably- but slowing down feels like failure, and failure feels worse than the pain.

Her limbs ache immediately. A deep, bone-level soreness that makes every step feel heavier than the last. She still feels empty, like someone hollowed her out and forgot to put anything back in. But the dizziness is gone. Her vision isn’t tunneling anymore. The world is steady.

That has to mean something.

She can do this.

She forces her legs forward, arms pumping mechanically as she focuses on form- heel, toe, breath, repeat. The burn in her lungs sharpens with every inhale, a raw scraping sensation that makes her throat sting. Her steps wobble, just slightly, like her knees might buckle if she lets her guard down.

Her heart pounds erratically, loud enough that she can hear it in her ears. Too fast. Way too fast.
Each step sends pain shooting up her calves and into her thighs, muscles screaming in protest.
Her lungs feel tight, compressed, squeezing harder every second.

She pushes forward anyway.

She rounds a corner and spots the finish line far ahead- the ribbon already snapped, scraps fluttering weakly against the tree. The crowd is clustered in the distance, a blur of color and motion and sound.

It looks impossibly far, but she pushes on, nonetheless.

Then pain explodes through her side, sharp and sudden, stealing the air straight from her lungs. She gasps, folding forward instinctively, one hand flying to her stomach as her heart slams violently against her ribs. It’s chaotic and overwhelming, drowning out everything else.

She tries to breathe.

Her lungs barely cooperate- short, shallow gasps that don’t do anything except make her chest hurt more.

She stumbles, hands braced on her thighs as she pants hard, face twisting as another wave of pain rolls through her, the side stitch intensifying.

Get up.
She tells herself.
Get the fuck up. You’re fine.

Her brain screams commands at her legs- stand, move, run- but her body refuses. It just stands there, shaking, heart still racing like it’s out of control. Tears well in her eyes, hot and humiliating, blurring her vision again.

You’re so fucking close.
Just run.
You’ve run farther on worse days.

Her stomach churns violently, the granola bar sitting heavy inside her. Nausea creeps up her throat, sharp and immediate, and guilt slams into her chest right after- brutal and familiar.

You shouldn’t have eaten.
You knew better.
You ruined it.

She looks up just in time to see runners pass her, sneakers pounding the pavement in steady, confident rhythms. Their faces are focused, determined- some strained, some smiling- but all of them are moving. None of them are stuck frozen in place like this.

She watches them go, helpless, heart still hammering out of sync with her breath.

Then a shadow crosses her vision.

Agatha.

Rio sees her out of focus at first- navy sweats, familiar stride- before her eyes sharpen just enough to track her. Agatha runs past, gaze flicking sideways for half a second. Their eyes meet.

Agatha’s brow furrows.

She keeps going.

Rio’s chest tightens painfully, something sinking low in her stomach.

Then Agatha slows.

Stops.

Rio watches her hesitate- sees her shoulders rise and fall with a sharp exhale, the way she looks toward the finish line like she’s weighing something. Then, with a quiet resignation, Agatha turns around.

She walks back.

Agatha jerks her head toward the end of the path. “Come on.”

She steps in close, slides her arm through Rio’s like it belongs there, and pulls her upright with surprising strength.

Normally, Rio would snap.
Pull away.
Say something biting to reassert distance, to remind them both of the lines Agatha drew.

But she’s too tired. Too wrung out to pretend she’s okay.

She shakes her head weakly, breath coming out broken. “I can’t.”

“Yes, you can,” Agatha says, immediate and firm, like the idea of Rio not finishing doesn’t even register.

Rio keeps her gaze fixed on the ground. “Agatha,” she sighs, a warning she doesn’t have the energy to enforce.

“Rio,” Agatha answers, still looking at her, still waiting.

Rio finally looks up.

Agatha’s face is close- too close. There’s sweat at her hairline, her cheeks flushed from exertion, her expression soft but stubborn. Determined in that infuriating way that makes Rio want to scream and cry at the same time.

Rio bites the inside of her cheek, grounding herself through the sharp sting.

Agatha tilts her head, lips quirking into a small, knowing smile. “Come on. You’re crossing that finish line if I have to drag you there myself.”

She steps forward, tugging Rio along with her.

They walk. Slowly.

Rio leans into Agatha more than she means to, arm locked tight, her free hand clutching Agatha’s sleeve like an anchor. Her breathing evens out bit by bit. The pain in her side dulls to a manageable ache. Her heart rate slowly begins to calm.

The finish line gets closer.

Step by step.

On the final stretch, something in Rio straightens. Pride, maybe. Stubbornness. The need to prove- something. She picks up her pace into a jog, ignoring the screaming protests from her legs.

She needs to finish running.
Even if it’s pathetic.
Even if it barely counts.

Agatha notices immediately and releases her arm, slowing without a word, letting Rio jog ahead of her.

Rio doesn’t look back. She can’t.

Her eyes lock on the finish line. She times her breaths. Forces her body into motion through sheer will.

So close.

She crosses the line of tape and stumbles to a stop as cheers swell around her. Her legs finally give out as she drifts toward a tree and slides down into the grass, chest heaving, vision blurring.

Wanda appears long enough to press a cold water bottle into her hands, whispering a quick congratulations before darting off again to attend to the other runners. Rio drinks greedily, water spilling down her chin.

Then she looks up.

Agatha sprints across the finish line moments later, effortless and fast, like the run barely touched her. Her eyes flick toward Rio for a brief second.

She offers a small, sad smile.

Then she disappears into the crowd, leaving Rio sitting there- sweating, wrecked, and aching in every possible way.

Billy strolls over and drops down beside her in the grass, the movement casual but hesitant, like he’s not sure he’s welcome there. Rio keeps her eyes fixed ahead, on nothing in particular, fingers tightening slightly around the water bottle in her hands.

“Hey,” he says.

Rio nods in response, a small flick of her chin. It’s automatic. Polite. Her stomach twists anyway, a slow pang of uneasiness settling low in her gut. She knows this tone. Knows this pause. Knows exactly where this is headed.

“Are you okay?” Billy asks, voice tripping over itself. “Like, regarding everything from earlier?”

Rio takes another sip of water, dragging the moment out, buying herself time. The liquid feels too cold in her mouth, sloshing uncomfortably in her stomach. She swallows hard and keeps her eyes forward.

“Mhm.”

It’s not convincing. She knows that. He knows that.

Billy hesitates, fingers picking at the grass beside him. “Are you starving yourself?”

The words hit her like a slap.

Rio’s breath catches sharply in her chest, air snagging halfway in. Her heart constricts, panic flaring hot and fast, like she’s been exposed under a harsh light she didn’t consent to. The question echoes in her head, loud and accusatory.

Too direct.
Too much.
Too real.

Subtlety is dead, apparently.

“I mean,” Billy rushes to add, clearly sensing he’s crossed some invisible line, “just cause of what I heard in the tent-”

Rio’s head snaps toward him, sharp and sudden. “You didn’t hear anything,” she snaps, voice brittle, edges cutting. “Nothing’s wrong. Got it?” Her glare is warning and desperate all at once, daring him to push further.

She hates how fast it came out.
Hates how much fear is packed into it.

Billy freezes. Swallows. His shoulders slump just slightly. “I’m just worried about you.”

“Don’t be.”

The words are immediate. Harsh. Final.

Billy exhales slowly, defeated. He nods once, then pushes himself up off the ground and walks away, giving her the space she was silently asking for.

The guilt hits instantly.

It blooms heavy and sharp in her chest, pressing against her ribs. For snapping. For shutting him down. For treating concern like an accusation. She watches his retreating back, jaw tight, throat burning.

That’s what she’s good at.
Pushing people away the moment they get too close.
The moment they start seeing things she works so hard to hide.

Tears prick at the corners of her eyes, sudden and unwelcome. She blinks hard, jaw clenching as she tries to rationalize it, tries to justify herself.

She doesn’t need more people knowing.
Doesn’t need the looks, the pity, the concern whispered like she’s fragile.

She has to keep this contained. Controlled.

That’s what she tells herself as she sits slumped against the tree, shoulders caving inward, exhaustion weighing her down. The calories from earlier feel too loud in her head, making her stomach twist and ache as it begs for more.

Her stomach growls softly, traitorously.

She ignores it.

She won’t give in again.

She can’t.
